The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
REFERENCE BY THE CHAIR 
 World Autism Awareness Day 
MR. CHAIRMAN :  Hon’ble Members, today, April 2 is observed globally as World 
Autism Awareness Day, as designated by the United Nations.  On this occasion, this 
House reiterates its commitment to safeguarding the rights and promoting the welfare 
of persons with autism. 
The United Nations theme for this year,  “Advancing Neurodiversity and the UN 
Sustainable Development Goals ”, emphasizes the need to recognize neurodiversity 
as an integral part of human diversity and to ensure that our developmental efforts are 
inclusive and sustainable. Autism Spec trum Disorder affects communication, 
behaviour and social interaction; howe ver, individuals on the spectrum possess 
distinctive strengths and capabilities which, with appropriate support and 
opportunities, can greatly enrich our society. 
India has put in place a robust legisl ative framework through the Rights of 
Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 and the National Trust Act, 1999, to secure rights 
and extend necessary support services. Nevertheless, laws and schemes achieve 
their true purpose only when their benefits reach every eligible is individual. Greater 
focus on early diagnosis, inclusive educat ion, skill training and awareness at the 
community level is essential to bridge existing gaps and promote equality. 
H o n ’ b l e  M e m b e r s ,  o n  t h i s  s i g n i f i c a n t  d a y ,  l e t  u s  r e a f f i r m  o u r  c o l l e c t i v e  
determination to foster an environment of understanding, acceptance and equal 
participation. An inclusive society is streng thened when every citizen, irrespective of 
ability, is given the opportunity to contribute and succeed.  It our shared responsibility 
to ensure that persons with autism are not merely accommodated, but fully 
empowered as valued members of our national community. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Message from Lok Sabha; Secretary-General.

ANNOUNCEMENT BY THE CHAIR 
MR. CHAIRMAN : Hon’ble Members, a Supplementary List of Business has been 
issued for consideration and passing of the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) 
Bill 2026, as passed by Lok Sabha. On e more Government Bill has been listed 
in today's Revised List of Business.  In order to dispose of the urgent Government 
Legislative Business, if the House agrees, the lunch hour and the  Private Members’ 
Resolutions may be dispensed with today.   
As the time allocation for these Bills has not been done by the Business 
Advisory Committee, I held discussions with Hon’ble Minister of Parliamentary Affairs 
and hon’ble leaders of parties and, as per consensus reached, the House may agree 
to allot two hours for the Andhra Prades h Reorganisation (Amendment) Bill, 2026, 
and three hours for the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 2026, as passed 
by Lok Sabha. 
The House has agreed to dispense with the lunch hour,  the Private Members’ 
Resolutions and time allocation for discussion on the Government Bills.   
Hon’ble Members, the copies of the Government Bills, as passed by the Lok 
Sabha, were circulated to the hon’ble Members yesterday evening.  The Members 
who desire to give notices of amendments to the said Bills may do so now till 12.00 
noon so as to enable the Secretariat to process and circulate the list of 
Amendments.  Notices received after 12.00 noon will, however, not be processed.

MATTERS RAISED WITH PERMISSION  
Need for expedited completion of Kawrpui River Bridge Project under India-
Bangladesh Agreement  
SHRI K. VANLALVENA (Mizoram) :  Sir, I rise today with deep gratitude to mention 
the ongoing construction of a bridge over th e Khawthlang Tuipui river, undertaken by 
the Ministry of Home Affairs. During my visi t to several Border Outposts of the Border 
Security Force along the Indo-Bangladesh border in Mizoram in 2021, I witnessed 
first-hand the immense challenges faced by our jawans and the local villagers. Many 
BOPs had no road connectivity due to abse nce of a bridge across the Khawthlang 
Tuipui river. Likewise, several villages in this border areas remained completely cut off 
from the rest of the country.  The officers of the BSF and the leaders of these villages 
shared with me their difficulties, especially during the rainy season when floods isolate 
the region for days. Tragically, innocent ch ildren have lost their lives because medical 
help could not reach them in time due to lack of roads and bridges. Moved by their 
16
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
plight, I submitted a request to the hon’bl e Union Home Minister for sanctioning the 
construction of a bridge near  Tlabung village over the Khaw thlang Tuipui river. I am 
grateful that the Ministry of Home Affairs approved this request and sanctioned Rs. 54 
crores for the project. The construction has now begun and is expected to be 
completed within two years.  Once completed, this bridge will be a lifeline for the poor 
and marginalized people living in these re mote border areas. It will connect them to 
the rest of Mizoram, open access to essent ial services, and improve their daily lives. 
At the same time, it will greatly assist our soldiers in ensuring security and in carrying 
out their duties more effectively. Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I place on record my heartfelt 
thanks to the hon’ble  Home Minister, Shri Amit Shah, for this generous and timely 
intervention in favour of Mizoram. This brid ge will stand as a lasting testament to the 
Government's commitment to both national  security and welfare of our border 
communities.  In the meantime, it is pertinent to recall that a bilateral agreement for 
establishing a Border Trade Centre at  Kawrpuichhuah was signed between the 
Governments of India and Bangladesh over ten years ago.  As per the agreement, a 
new bridge was to be constructed across the Kawnpui River to connect the two 
countries and facilitate trade and cooperation.  However, despite the passage of a 
decade since the signing of this agreement,  no concrete steps have been taken to 
implement the project, leaving the commitment  unfulfilled to this day. Therefore, I 
humbly request the Union Government to  initiate without further delay the 
construction of the proposed bridge acr oss the Kawnpui River, along with all 
necessary supporting infrastructure, as envisaged under the agreement signed with 
the Bangladesh Government.  Timely action on this matter will not only honour the 
commitment made over a decade ago but also strengthen bilateral trade, connectivity 
and cooperation between the two nations.                                                                                    
MR. CHAIRMAN : The following hon’ble Members associated themselves with the 
matter raised by the hon’ble Member, Shri K. Vanlalvena:    
Shri Maharaja Sanajaoba Leishemba (Manip ur), Shri P. Wilson (Tamil Nadu), 
Shri Niranjan Bishi (Odisha) and Dr. Sasmit Patra (Odisha).

Concern over service uncertainty and pending financial dues of Government 
employees in Andhra Pradesh 
SHRI SUBHAS CHANDRA BOSE PILLI (Andhra Pradesh) : Sir, there is growing 
insecurity among Government employees in Andhra Pradesh under the present 
Government, particularly in the light of the proposed rule, mandating compulsory 
retirement after 33 years of service. This would effectively force many employees to 
retire around the age of 58 despite the noti fied retirement age being 62 years, thereby 
depriving them nearly four years of service and consequential financial and pensionary 
benefits. 
  Simultaneously, major financial commi tments remain pending including Pay 
Revision Commission (PRC) benefits, Interim Relief, Dearness Allowance arrears and 
substantial hospital reimbursement claims under the Employees Health Scheme, with

Now, Shriman A. D. Singh. 
Concern over shortfall in transfer of cess collections to designated funds 
SHRI A. D. SINGH (Bihar) : Sir, the Comptroller and Au ditor General has revealed 
that as of 2023-24, the Central Government failed to transfer Rs. 3.69 lakh crore 
worth of such collection to the designated funds for which they were levied. These 
shortfalls span several decades, beginning in 1974 and continue to more recent years. 
The largest shortfall relates to Oil Indu stry Development Board, wherein between 
1974-75 and 1991-92, an amount of Rs . 902.40 crore was transferred but, 
subsequently, amounts were not fully transfe rred, leaving a total shortfall of Rs. 2.95 
crore up to the financial year 2023-24.  An other major area of shortfall was the Health 
and Education Cess. The levy was not fully transferred to the designated funds.  
The CAG reported that between 2018-19 and 2023-24, the Centre did not 
transfer Rs.37,537 crore.  Other funds impacted include the Investor Education and 
Protection Fund (Rs.2,505.5 crore) and the Monetisation of National Highways Fund 
(Rs.5,968.1 crore).  While the Finance Mini stry claimed that Rs.3.66 lakh crore had 
been transferred between 2018-19 and 2023-24, the CAG stressed the need for 
reconciliation since the Government accounts reflected only Rs.2.65 lakh crore.  The 
findings highlight persistent lapses in channeling cess proceeds towards their 
intended developmental purposes.  The pe ople of this country are entitled to 
transparency and assurance that their contri butions through cesses are utilized solely 
for the intended developmental and welfare objectives.  This House should know as 
to why cess collections were not duly transferred to the designated funds and what 
immediate steps the Government has taken to ensure that cess proceeds are 
allocated to their rightful purposes.

Shriman Nabam Rebia. 
Demand to include Arunachal Pradesh in the 6th Schedule and amendment of 
Article 371-H of the Constitution of India  
SHRI NABAM REBIA (Arunachal Pradesh) : Thank you, Chairman, Sir, for allowing 
me to raise a very important issue of my Stat e, Arunachal Pradesh.  This is regarding, 
‘Inclusion of Arunachal Pradesh in the Sixth Schedule and Amendment of Article 371-
H of the Constitution.’ This issue has been  raised many times on the floor of this 
House and also in the other House.  The Legislative Assembly of Arunachal Pradesh 
has also adopted many times resolutions, and such resolutions have been forwarded 
to the Government. Now, today's Arunachal Pradesh, immediately after 
Independence, was administered by the Government through the Ministry of External 
Affairs. Later, it became a Union Territory and then, in 1987, Arunachal Pradesh 
became a State under Article 371-H of the Constitution. It is because all the Tribal 
States of North-East, including the Autonomous District Councils, have been 
included under Sixth Schedule, and Arunach al Pradesh, which is a purely Tribal 
State, has been left out. So, we are demanding that Arunachal Pradesh should be 
included under the Sixth Schedule to the Constitution. If it is included in the Sixth 
Schedule to the Constitution, the protection that Arunachal Pradesh will be getting is, 
it will have protection over religious or so cial practices of the tribal people of 
Arunachal Pradesh, customary law and procedure of the tribes of Arunachal Pradesh, 
administration of civil and criminal justic e, ownership and transfer of land and its 
resources.   
Sir, we have been demanding it.  Now, how long will the Government take to 
resolve this issue? Article 371-H gives official powers, provisions to the Centre 
through the Governor of Arunachal Pradesh.  This Article gives Governor 
extraordinary power over the law and order in  the State. Importantly, decisions under 
this Article cannot be questioned. Therefore, it is our urgent request to the 
Government. The hon’ble Minister for Parlia mentary Affairs, probably, when he was 
the Member of the other House, also raised this issue. This is a serious issue and we 
want the Government of India to take initiative to amend the Constitution and to bring 
Arunachal, to include Arunachal under the Sixth Schedule of the Constitution.  
MR. CHAIRMAN : The following hon’ble Members associated themselves with the 
matter raised by the hon’ble Member, Shri Nabam Rebia:

DR. FAUZIA KHAN (Maharashtra): Sir, I would like to draw urgent attention to a silent 
financial crisis afflicting crores of middle-cl ass families across India. It is the ruthless 
lapse rules governing life and health insurance policies. Today, most insurance 
policies including those under LIC provide a mere 30-day grace period for premium 
payment. A single missed instalment caused by sudden job loss, medical emergency, 
delayed salary or temporary cash flow shortfall is enough to collapse an entire policy. 
Years of premium paid faithfully become worthless overnight. Revival demands, steep 
penalties, compounded interest, fresh medi cal examinations and, sometimes, even 
fresh underwriting. It punishes precisely th e people when they are most vulnerable. 
The very people who have built LIC into the institution are the salaried middle class, 
factory workers, teachers, small traders and gig economy workers. Sir, they pay their 
premium year after year honestly trusting their families will be protected, but one 
single month of financial difficulty, that can erase their trust and the cover entirely. In a 
post pandemic economy  where income disruptions are increasingly common, such 
rigidities are unfair and inhumane. Insurance was designed to stand beside families in 
distress.  It must not abandon them because of this, Sir.  I, therefore, urge this 
Government and IRDAI to urgently implement three pro-consumer reforms.  First, 
extend the grace period from 30 days to 90 days.  Second, replace all or ‘nothing 
lapse’ rules with professional late fees, so that only the missed period attracts a 
reasonable penalty.  And, third, allow auto matic revival within one month to 80 days 
on payment plus nominal late charge with out requiring fresh medical examination or 
fresh underwriting and all.   They are bare  minimum that crores of honest policy 
holders deserve.  I urge the hon’ble Finance Minister to direct IRDAI to notify these 
reforms.  Sir, I will end my speech with one couplet by Mirza Ghalib.   
“न सुनो गर बुरा कहे कोई, 
न कहो गर बुरा करे कोई। 
रोक लो गर ग़लत चले कोई, 
बĪश दो गर ख़ता करे कोई।”

Need to compliment the Government for the significant reduction in fuel excise  
duty amid the West Asia conflict 
SHRI MAHARAJA SANAJAOBA LEISHEMBA (Manipur):  Sir, my Zero Hour subject is 
‘Need to compliment the Government for significant reduction in fuel excise duty amid 
West Asia conflict.’ I rise to show gratit ude to hon’ble Prime Minister, Shri Narendra 
Modi, and his Government for the bold decision to shield its citizens from rising global 
fuel price by sharply reducing excise duties on petrol from Rs.13 to Rs.3 per litre and 
diesel from Rs.10 to zero or nil per litre.  Amid escalating tensions in West Asia and a 
steep surge in crude oil prices from around  $70 per barrel to nearly $122 per barrel in 
just a month, fuel prices have risen across the world.  Fuel price hikes in South-East 
Asian countries is 30 to 50 per cent, North America around 30 per cent, Europe about 
20 per cent and Africa close to 50 per cent.  Only India stands out as a rare exception. 
Faced with this global volatility, the Modi  Government chose to absorb the financial 
burden rather than pass it on to the citizens.  This move not only reduces pressure on 
oil companies, but also ensures that there is  effectively no additional burden on the 
common man.  Despite significant revenue implications, the Government has 
prioritized public welfare, continuing it s consistent approach since the Russian-
Ukraine conflict began by insulating citize ns from global price shocks.  Additional 
export duties have been imposed at Rs.21.5 per litre on diesel and Rs.29.5 per litre on 
ATF to ensure adequate domestic availability and prevent undue profiteering amid 
high international prices. This timely and bold decision of  Modi Government reflects a 
clear commitment to economic stability and citizen welfare in challenging global 
circumstances.  Really, it is a big relief for the 140 crore citizens of India. I, therefore, 
urge all the hon’ble Members of this Uppe r House to show our gratitude to hon’ble 
Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, and hi s Government for this bold and a citizen-
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
29

 
 
centric decision and let us stand unitedly duri ng this critical global crisis.  Thank you, 
Sir, for giving me the opportunity of raising my last Zero Hour today.

Shrimati Renuka Chowdhury. 
Need to expedite pending Central Government projects in Telangana  
SHRIMATI RENUKA CHOWDHURY (Telangana) : Thank you very much, Sir, for 
permitting me to draw attention of this House to a matter of urgent public importance. 
Several railway projects are pending clearance in Telangana despite the completion of 
all surveys and submission of Detailed Project Reports. As a result, these areas 
remain unconnected. These include new rail way lines such as Dornakal-Miryalguda, 
Ramagundam-Manuguru, Karimnagar-Hasanparthi, Bhadrachalam-Kovvur, 
Patancheru-Adilabad and Bodh an-Latur. In addition, key capacity expansion works 
like doubling of Vikarabad-Parli, doubling of Mudkhed-Pimpalkutti and 
tripling/quadrupling of Kazipet-Secunderab ad and Wadi-Guntakal sections are also 
pending approval. Sir, when the Government, despite submitting all papers, keeps 
these issues pending, it affects passenger connectivity.  We have freight corridors. 
W e  t a l k  o f  f o o d  s e c u r i t y .   H o w  d o  y o u  transport foodgrains, support industrial 
structures and connect backward regions in the State?  Despite their strategic 
importance, delays in approvals have stalled progress and limited the State’s growth 
potential.   We are a new State and this shou ld have been on top priority. We talk of 
smart cities!  Besides all this, Sir, there is a huge cost overrun that gets involved, for 
which the Central Government stands no guarantee. The concern is further 
compounded by the non-fulfilment of commitments under the Andhra Pradesh 
Reorganisation Act, particularly, the Rail Ma nufacturing Unit at Kazipet, that remains 
pending even after more than a decade. Almost 12 years have passed since the 
assurance was given in this House.  So, what is the sanctity of giving such 
assurances and the constitutional provisions if we do not respect them and we keep 
States suffering like this? Sir, I urge the Government, through you, to expedite 
approvals of these pending railway pr ojects. There should be time-bound

urge the Government to fulfil the commit ments made under the AP Reorganisation

12.00 Noon  
ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Coverage of National Mission for Green India 
#*406.  SHRI CHUNNILAL GARASIYA: Will the Minister of ENVIRONMENT, FOREST 
AND CLIMATE CHANGE be pleased to state: 
(a) whether Government is aware of decline in tree plantation activities 
recorded under the National Mission for Green India during the period 
between 2015 and 2025; 
(b) if so, the steps being taken by the Ministry to address the shortfalls and 
accelerate plantation activities under the Mission; 
(c) the specific role of the weightage given by the 16th Finance Commission in 
encouraging States to increase their forest cover; 
(d) whether the Ministry has successfully integrated carbon sequestration 
monitoring into the design of the Mission; and 
(e) if so, the details thereof? 
                   
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
37

 
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF ENVIRONMENT, FOREST AND 
CLIMATE CHANGE   (SHRI KIRTIVARDHAN SINGH) : (a) to (e) A Statement is laid 
on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b)  The National Mission for a Green India (NMGI) is one of the nine 
Missions under the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) and aims to 
protect, restore and enhance forest and tree cover while contributing to climate 
change mitigation and adaptation through plantation and ecosystem restoration in 
identified landscapes. The Green India Mi ssion (GIM), a scheme under the NMGI, 
adopts a landscape-based approach, wh erein interventions are planned and 
implemented across contiguous forest and non-forest areas, integrating ecological, 
hydrological, and socio-economic dimensio ns. Activities under GIM are undertaken 
based on approved Perspective Plans an d Annual Plans of Operation (APOs) 
submitted by States/UTs and approved by the National Executive Council. The 
Mission is implemented through a conv ergence-based framework, leveraging 
resources from various Central and State Government schemes and programmes.  
GIM being a long-term mission focused on ecosystem restoration, 
afforestation, and climate adaptation, plantation and eco-restoration activities under 
GIM may vary across the years due to factor s such as phased approvals of plans, 
timely availability of funds, and institutional preparedness at the State level. GIM has 
contributed to the overall increase in India’s forest and tree cover. As per the India 
State of Forest Report (ISFR), forest and tree cover has increased from 7,94,245 sq 
km in 2015 to 8,27,356.95 sq km in 2023, registering a net increase of 33,111.95 sq 
km. These gains are attributable to sustained afforestation and eco-restoration efforts 
undertaken through coordinated actions of Central and State Governments under 
various schemes, including GIM.  
Further, under the overarching theme of “Ek Ped Maa Ke Naam”, a nationwide 
voluntary plantation initiative was launched on 5th June 2024 to promote tree planting 
through public participation and convergence with ongoing schemes. 
To address gaps and accelerate implementation, the Ministry has taken several 
measures, including; Strengthening planni ng processes with improved technical 
support; Promoting convergence with Central and State schemes and programmes of 
line departments; Streamlining fund flow  mechanisms and enhancing transparency 
38
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
through digital monitoring t ools; and Encouraging adoption  of site-specific, climate-
resilient plantation models.     
(c) The 16th Finance Commission has been mandated to recommend the 
distribution of central taxes among States taking into account several factors 
including forest cover. The Commission in its report has relied upon the framework 
that uses forest and tree cover data (as per India State of Forest Reports), including 
Very Dense, Moderately Dense and Open Forest categories, for incentivizing States. 
This approach aligns fiscal devolution with  national priorities and provides financial 
resources to States to support afforestation, ecological restoration, and sustainable 
forest management, complementing initiatives like the Green India Mission. 
(d) and (e) Monitoring of forest and tree cover, including assessment of forest 
carbon stock, is carried out biennially by the Forest Survey of India (FSI)  using 
remote sensing, GIS-based tools, and ground-based inventory methods. These 
assessments capture the impact of afforestation and restoration activities undertaken 
under various schemes, includ ing GIM. Further, the Ministry promotes the use of 
technology-based monitoring tools , including geo-tagging, remote sensing, and 
field-based verification, to improve accuracy of plantation monitoring.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Q.No.407.  
Bio-diversity awareness 
*407. SHRIMATI SANGEETA YADAV : Will the Minister of ENVIRONMENT, FOREST 
AND CLIMATE CHANGE be pleased to state: 
(a) whether Government is promoting bio-diversity awareness through bio-
diversity-related activities in biodiversity rich areas; 
(b) if so, the details thereof; and 
(c) the details of steps taken in this direction during the last three years, State- 
wise, including Uttar Pradesh? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF ENVIRONMENT, FOREST AND 
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
CLIMATE CHANGE (SHRI KIRTIVARDHAN SINGH): (a) to (c) A statement is laid on 
the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (c) Yes, during the last three ye ars, the Government of India has taken 
several steps to promote biodiversity awareness in the various States and UTs 
including Uttar Pradesh through implementati on of Biological Diversity Act, 2002 (as 
amended in 2023). The Act provides for development of national and state level 
strategies and plans for conservation, promotion and sustainable use of biological 
diversity. These include measures for identi fication and monitoring of areas rich in 
biological resources, promotion of in-situ, and ex-situ, conservation of biological 
resources, including cultivars, folk variet ies and landraces, incentives for research, 
training and public education to  increase awareness with respect to biodiversity. 
         Biodiversity awareness is prom oted through a decentralized and participatory 
three-tier institutional mechanism comprisi ng the National Biod iversity Authority 
(NBA) at national level, and State Biodiver sity Boards/ Union Territory Biodiversity 
Councils at State/ UT level and Biodiversity Management Committees at grassroot 
level, ensuring effective outreach and community involvement. NBA in coordination 
with SBBs/ UTBCs facilitated the constitution of Biodiversity Management 
Committees across the Country, which play a key role in grassroot-level awareness 
and conservation activities, along with preparation of People’s Biodiversity Registers 
(PBRs) at local level to document biodiver sity and associated traditional knowledge, 
thereby enhancing awareness and community participation. 
         Pursuant to the Biological Diversity Act, 2002 (as amended in 2023), the 
Biological Diversity (Access to Biological Resources and Knowledge Associated 
thereto and Fair and Equitable Sharing of Be nefits) Regulations, 2025 was notified on 
29 April 2025, superseding the Access and Benefit Sharing guidelines 2014.  As on 
date, National Biodiversity Authority coll ected Access and Benefit Sharing amount to 
the tune of Rs. 263.00 Crores, of which Rs. 145 Crores have been disbursed through 
the State Biodiversity Boards/Union Territory  Biodiversity Councils to the farmers, 
Biodiversity Management Committees and other benefit claimers. The benefit-sharing 
amount is utilized for supporting conservation activities and promoting socio-
economic development in the areas from wh ere the biological resources have been 
accessed. 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
45

 
 
        The International Day for Biological Diversity is observed annually on 22 May at 
the national and State/ UT levels by organizing various outreach activities for students 
and local communities to enhance awareness regarding biodiversity conservation. 
       In the State of Uttar Pradesh, biodiversity conservation is being promoted 
through training and capacity building pr ogrammes for various stakeholders including 
BMCs and Panchayat f unctionaries. BMCs have been constituted in the State at 
grassroots level. In addition, the Forest Department of Uttar Pradesh, undertook a 
large-scale plantation drive in 2025, planti ng more than 35 crore saplings under the 
Plantation Maha Abhiyan 2025 and Ek Ped Maa Ke Naam campaign. 
        India updated and released its Nati onal Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan – 
(2024-2030) at the 16th meeting of the Conference of the Parties to the Convention 
on the Biological Diversity (CBD CoP-16) in  Cali, Colombia, on 30th October 2024 in 
alignment with the Kunming Montreal Global  Biodiversity Framew ork, reaffirming its 
leadership among developing countries. Th e updated National Biodiversity Strategy 
and Action Plan – (2024-2030) develo ped through ‘Whole-o f-Government’ and 
‘Whole-of-Society’ approach, emphasizes public awareness and outreach on 
biodiversity conservation, particularly among youth and local communities. Key 
initiatives include integration of biodiversi ty into educational curricula, promotion of 
eco-clubs, awareness campaigns, and digital outreach through social media and 
mobile applications. 
         Biodiversity awareness activiti es are also undertaken through various schemes 
and programmes of the Ministry.These prog rammes actively engage communities, 
promote sustainable practices, and enhance understanding of ecosystem protection, 
thereby transforming biodiversity awarenes s into a participatory and people-driven 
movement. 
        The National Museum of Natural History under MoEFCC and its regional centres, 
are undertaking environmental awarene ss programmes/ activities like poster, 
painting, slogan and essay competitions , debates, seminars/ workshops for all 
sections of the society. Summer vacation programmes and winter nature camps are 
also organised for schoolchildren.  
         In addition, various organisations/  institutions under MoEF CC play vital role in 
conservation, research, and outreach activities.

YUva VIgyani KAryakram (YUVIKA) 
*408.  SHRI RYAGA KRISHNAIAH: Will the PRIME MINISTER be pleased to state: 
(a) the number of YUVIKA programmes co nducted since 2023, including year-
wise details of student participation, State/UT coverage and ISRO centres 
involved; 
(b) the broad thematic areas, hands-on  modules and outreach activities 
covered under the programme and their alignment with emerging trends in 
space science and technology; 
(c) whether any steps are being taken to expand the scale, regional outreach 
and institutional partnerships of YUVIKA  to encourage wider participation in 
future editions; and 
(d) if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE DEPARTMENT OF SPACE (DR. JITENDRA  
SINGH): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) Three editions of the YUVIKA programme have been conducted since 
2023, one each year in 2023, 2024, and 2025. 
Following are the details of number of students participated in each year’s 
YUVIKA programme. 
Year No.of Students 
2023 350 
2024 350 
2025 350 
 
On average, 10 students are selected from each State and 8 from each Union 
Territory. 
YUVIKA programme is hosted at seven ISRO Centres, namely Vikram Sarabhai 
Space Centre (VSSC), Satish Dhawan Space Centre SHAR (SDSC SHAR), National 
Remote Sensing Centre (NRSC), U R Rao Satellite Centre (URSC), Space 
Applications Centre (SAC), Indian Institute of Remote Sensing (IIRS), and North 
Eastern Space Applications Centre (NESAC). 
50
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
(b) Students are taught about ba sic space science, technology and 
applications. Projects and experiments including assembly and launch of a model 
rocket using DIY kit, creating simple cardboard models of Chandrayaan-3, 
assembling and flying a simple drone using DIY kit. Other activities include visits to 
nearby industries near the hosting ISRO Cent res, providing students with exposure to 
real-life industrial challenges. 
These elements align with emerging trends in space science and technology by 
fostering practical skills in rocketry. 
(c) and (d) Yes. Steps are being taken to expand the scale and regional 
outreach of the YUVIKA programme to encourage wider participation in future 
editions. To expand the programme, action is being taken to include two additional 
ISRO Centres to host programme. ISRO Centres across the country provide wide 
publicity to the programme to promote greater participation. At present, there are no 
plans for institutional partnerships to further expand the programme. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  First Supplementary, Shriman Ryaga Krishnaiah.   
SHRI RYAGA KRISHNAIAH : Sir, the YUVIKA programme is a highly appreciable 
programme. It will create innovative ideas and research mentality among the students  
at an early age.  I would like to know whether the Department has undertaken any 
assessment at the participation level from Andhra Pradesh, Telangana and other 
States since 2023; the details of student participation of the State, including year-
wise and district-wise break-up; any disparity in participation between urban areas 
and rural areas of Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.                             
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Hon’ble Minister.   
DR. JITENDRA SINGH:  Sir, I am glad that the hon’ble Member has put across a very 
important aspect of this question which is listed against his name.  It is Yuva Vigyan 
Karyakram (YUVIKA).  This is, in fact, a unique programme of its kind.  I think for the 
first time the Government has conceptualized  that.  And it was launched in the year 
2 0 1 9 .   T o  b e  v e r y  f r a n k ,  i t  w a s  p r o m p t e d  b y  t h e  a d v i c e  o f  h o n ’ b l e   P r i m e  M i n i s t e r  
Narendra Modi ji who has been right from day one emphasizing on providing equal 
opportunities, level-playing field and op timum access to young students across the 
sections and across the country.  For the be nefit of other hon’ble Members, Sir, let 
me also clarify that this programme envisages including students from class 9.  It is a 
dedicated programme meant for students of class 9 studying in schools either under 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
51

 
 
the Central Board or in the respective State Board.  Having said that, now, I come to 
addressing his concern.   If he goes through the details of the programme, he would 
realize that this programme has already been  designed in a fashion to give preference 
to rural students.  Now, I come to the criteria of eligibility that we have.  First of all, let 
me clarify that we select ten students mi nimum from each State and eight students 
minimum from each Union Territory.  We ha ve 28 States and 8 Union Territories.  The 
total number is 280 plus 64; ten students from each State and eight students from 
each Union Territory.  So, it is a very democratic manner of selection.  Even in the 
eligibility criteria, we have fixed 15 per cent marks for the schools which fall under a 
notified panchayat.  So the concern has already been addressed.   
Now, I come to his second question about the benefit accruing to Andhra 
Pradesh.  As I said, it has been a very democratic exercise.  We have so far identified 
seven centres across the country.  This is an ongoing process.  Out of those seven 
centres, one is Satish Dhawan Space Centre which is in Sriharikota, Andhra Pradesh.  
Another centre is in Hyderabad, which is the erstwhile Andhra Pradesh.  These 
centres encourage students to reside there for two months and undergo specialised 
training to acquaint themselves in the nu ances of space technology and to enable 
them to discover their hidden aptitude.  If you want, I can tell the names of the 
centres.  These are:  Indian Institute of Remote Sensing, Dehradun; Vikram Sarabhai 
Space Centre, Thiruvananthapuram, Kerala; Satish Dhavan Space Centre, Andhra 
Pradesh; UR Rao Satellite Centre, Karnataka; Space Application Centre, 
Ahmedabad; National Remote Sensing Centre, Hyderabad; and North Eastern Space 
Applications Centre  in Shillong.  Two more , we are going to include from this year - 
Mahendragiri in Tamil Nadu and Jodhpur in Rajasthan.  The course is conducted in 
the month of May, because that presumably is the vacation time.  Similarly, on the 
instructions of hon’ble Prime Minister, we have also launched a couple of other 
programmes for the adolescent students following the principle of Catch Them 
Young.  Very recently, after this, we also started a programme called Vigyan Jyoti.  
This is a dedicated programme for girls from  class 9 to class 11.  Not only this, we 
also then level them up at different stages as they grow up.  Very recently, we started 
a dedicated venture capital fund for space start-ups which is called Antariksh Venture 
Capital Fund with the corpus of one crore rupees.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Hon’ble Minister. 
DR. JITENDRA SINGH: Mr. Chairman, Sir, as the hon’ble  Member rightly pointed out 
and add to what he says, as I already mentioned, the rural students are already being 
given preference and also to schools which fall under Central Board or the State 
Board, which means most of them are Government schools. And not only that; the 
selection process starts somewhere around the month of January or February, 
concludes by the end of April and, in May, we conduct this one-month programme. 
Now, prior to the selection process itself, the pre-course or the pre-training period 
also is a kind of a formative period and includes all that the hon’ble Member has 
expressed. For example, 100 per cent criteria for selection of students include 10 per 
cent for quiz performances. So, quiz competitions are conducted across the country. 
Then, you have Science Fairs and studen ts are also judged at the level of 
participation. Two per cent for schools; 5 per cent for participation if the Science Fair 
is at the District level and 10  per cent if it is at the State level. Then, we also conduct 
Olympiads at the District level, at the State level and at the national level. Accordingly, 
2, 4 and 5 per cent respectively are the marks. Similarly, there is dedicated 5 per cent 
for Scouts and NSS. So, what I am trying to say is that the selection criteria itself is 
very wholesome keeping that in mind and gi ving preference to the overall background 
of the student, his attributes and then channelizing him. The results have been 
miraculous.  
Sir, if I can just take one minute.  We started off this entire new space reforms 
programme just about five or six year s back, with opening up of the Space 
Department to the non-Government players.  We were just about dismal economy. 
Today, we stand at nearly 9 billion US dollars and the pace of our rise is so fast that 
the projections predict that by 2033 we might go up to 44 or 45 billion US dollars. In 
the last 10-11 years -- this gives me an opportunity, just in a single sentence, to point 
out -- the total number of foreign satellites  launched so far in India, ever since we 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
53

 
 
began doing so, is 434. Out of that, 399,  which means more than 90 per cent, have 
been launched during the Modi regime, that is, after 2014, in just 11 years. So, there 
are 90 per cent of the foreign satellites, which have, in turn, yielded a tremendous 
amount of revenue for the State exchequer. For example, if the total revenue 
generated for the European satellites is 32 3 million Euros, out of that, 290 million 
Euros have been generated over the last 10 years alone. Similarly, as far as earning of 
foreign exchange is concerned, if 233 million US dollars were earned ever since the 
programme began some 3-4 decades back, a total amount of 218 million US dollars 
was earned in just one decade. So, you can understand the kind of emphasis the 
Modi Government has given to the Space Department. It has virtually become a very 
important component of India's overall economic growth and is going to play a very 
important role when we envisage India-2047.

SHRIMATI RAJATHI:  Respected Chairman, Sir, I would like to know whether there 
are any dedicated initiatives proposed to mentor young school girls and women to

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Hon’ble Minister.   
DR. JITENDRA SINGH:   Sir, I am glad the hon’ble Member has asked that. One, of 
course, YUVIKA does not make gender discrimination.  It is as much available to the 
girl students as much as the male students.  Now, this is a part of a larger ecosystem 
which the Government has sought to develop over the last one decade. It also 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
55

 
 
includes a number of other programmes dedi cated to girls from different age groups. 
As I said, the Vigyan Jyoti begins at class IX to XII.  Then, we have programmes at the 
pre-graduate stage.  We provide fellowsh ips that is called WISE-KIRAN programme.  
This Government has been very sensitive and thoughtful. It is also introducing mid 
career programmes.  Many of our young daughters give up their career midway for 
family reasons. They want to come back later in life. Earlier on, there was no such 
provision. We have provided that too as well. So, I would rather go one step further to 
say that the National Education Policy, 2020 -- though not a part of this Ministry but a 
part of the Education Ministry – is also a part of this larger ecosystem because it 
gives the student a choice to change over hi s subjects when he or she realizes that 
she has undergone the discovery of a different  kind of a talent or knack or aptitude 
inside him.  So , a student is no longer a prisoner of the subjects chosen by his 
parents or his guardians. For example, a pa rent thinks that a child should become a 
doctor. It prompts him to choose medical subjects. He does not make it to a medical 
school. Then, where does he go? He woul d end up doing B.Sc., in Bio Sciences, 
then M.Sc., and then PhD.  So, all will be happening by default. It is no longer so. 
Suppose, you wanted to become a doctor, you do not happen to, then you can 
change over to space technology, which was not happening earlier. This is a wider 
choice. As I said, there is a whole democratization of the education system and also a 
huge, huge prioritization giving to the inhe rent skills and the inherent aptitudes and 
also allowing the liberty to the aptitude to evolve. Some of the adolescents begin to 
realize that they had a different attitude. Like, for example, when you go to the film 
industry in Mumbai, everybody thinks I am handsome enough to become a hero.  
But, later on, they are made to realize that you could be only a scriptwriter or just be a 
clapper boy. So, it is like that. I think, there is a whole, whole opening up of this entire 
ecosystem for which hon’ble Prime Minister, Modi ji, has also been encouraging a 
whole of Government approach.

60
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Assessment of marine living resources 
*410. SHRI ASHOKRAO SHANKARRAO CHAVAN: Will the Minister of EARTH 
SCIENCES be pleased to state: 
(a)  whether the Ministry is undertakin g assessment and monitoring of marine 
living resources in India's Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ) and if so, the 
details thereof;  
(b)  the number of scientific surveys conducted during the last three years for 
estimation of fishery resources and marine biodiversity; 
(c) whether the data generated through these surveys are being used to 
support sustainable fisheries management and marine conservation and if 
so, the details thereof; and 
(d) the steps taken by the Ministry to  strengthen scientific research and 
monitoring of marine ecosystems in Indian waters? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MINISTRY OF EARTH SCIENCES (DR. JITENDRA 
SINGH): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the table of the House. 
Statement  
(a) Yes Sir. The Centre for Marine Li ving Resources and Ecology (CMLRE), 
an attached office of the Ministry of Earth Sciences, has been established with the 
primary mandate of assessment and monitoring  of marine living resources in India’s 
Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ) and for deve loping management strategies for their 
conservation. The programmes under which this work is being undertaken are as 
follows: 
i. Marine Living Resources (MLR) Prog ramme of CMLRE for survey, assessment 
and monitoring of marine living resources in the Indian EEZ.  
ii. Deep Ocean Mission (DOM), under which CMLRE is the nodal agency for 
exploration and conservation of deep -sea biodiversity in India’s EEZ, 
particularly in biodiversity hotspots such as seamounts.  
iii. In addition, ICAR-Central Marine Fi sheries Research Institute (ICAR-CMFRI), 
Kochi is also conducting comprehensive and continuous programmes for 
assessment and monitoring of marine living resources across India’s territorial 
waters and EEZ. The assessment and monitoring framework operated by 
ICAR-CMFRI rests on two distinct but complementary pillars: 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
61

 
 
(a) Landings based Production Monitoring 
(b) Life History Traits based Stock Assessment 
(b) A total of 22 research cruises were conducted during the last three 
years for estimation of fishery-resource- related parameters an d marine resource 
assessment. 17 research cruises were undertaken in the Bay of Bengal and Arabian 
Sea, 1 cruise was taken in the Indian Ocean region and 2 cruises each were 
undertaken in the Lakshadweep and Andama n seas respectively. These scientific 
cruises were done for mapping the living and fisheries resource assessment. In 
addition, the Fisheries Survey of India (FSI) and ICAR-CMFRI conduct annual stock 
assessment surveys and provide national status reports such as National Stock 
assessment (2022) and the Indian Ocean T una Commission (IOTC) linked fisheries 
data submissions (2023-24). 
(c) Yes Sir. The data generated through these surveys are being utilized for 
fishery resource and habitat assessment, id entification of biodiversity hotspots and 
spawning periods/breeding grounds of fi sh species, ecosystem studies including 
ocean acidification, and development of biodiversity/genetic databases, thereby 
supporting sustainable fisheries management and marine conservation. Further, the 
Marine Living Resources programme of CM LRE incorporates advanced technologies 
and multidisciplinary approaches, thereby improving monitoring systems and enabling 
better assessment of ocean health. Thes e efforts support evidence-based decision-
making for conservation and sustainable use of marine resources. CMLRE employs 
advanced and innovative biodiversity assessment techniques, including environmental 
DNA (eDNA) metabarcoding and genomic analyses, alongside conventional 
sampling methods. These approaches enab le both qualitative and quantitative 
assessments of biodiversity. 
(d) The steps taken by the Ministry to strengthen scientific research 
includes implementation of the Deep Ocean Mission for deep-sea biodiversity 
exploration and conservation including: 
i. Use of the FORV Sagar Sampada fo r multidisciplinary marine research 
ii. Strengthening of ocean observation syst ems through glider missions, drifters   
and Argo floats deployments 
iii. Augmentation of marine biodiversi ty databases through IndOBIS and related 
scientific capacity-building efforts. 
62
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
In addition, ‘Bhavasagara’ is a designat ed national-level Marine Biodiversity 
Referral Centre established at CMLRE, Kochi. It acts as a repository for deep-sea 
faunal collections from the Indian Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ). At present, the 
repository houses a total of 3,560 voucher specimens, of which more than 70% 
represent deep-water and open-ocean species. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Third supplementary; Dr. Sasmit Patra. 
DR. SASMIT PATRA : Sir, my specific query is on Olive Ridley turtles that come and 
nest off the coast of Odisha.  Sir, through you, I would like to ask the Minister that 
over the various years, over decades, hist orically, off the coast of Odisha, we have 
Olive Ridley turtles that come, nest, hatch and along with their little children, they also 
again go back into the Bay of Bengal. It is  a very beautiful sight to see. Over the 
years, because of trollers and shipping vehi cles, many of these Olive Ridley turtles, 
while swimming back from the coast of Odisha get caught in the nets of these 
shipping vehicles, get caught in the propel lers of these small trollers and shipping 
vehicles and die. Some of them are also poac hed as they go back into the sea.  Sir, 
my specific question is: What is the Ministry doing in terms of trying to protect them?  
Thank you. 
DR. JITENDRA SINGH:  Sir, hon’ble Member is a well read person. Even though the 
question is not per se  related to the main question asked and I could have ordinarily 
very easily skipped out of that, but, I would like to try to respond to my friend to the 
best of my knowledge.  I think, precisely, what you are saying is the concern not only 
on the shores of Odisha, but as much on the Arabian Sea and the Western Ghats as 
well.  That is why, in 2026, very thoughtf ully, as a part of Deep Ocean Mission, -- 
again, I have no hesitation to say that  it was the hon’ble Prime Minister, who 
announced from the ramparts of Red Fort, not once but twice, in his Independence 
Address in 2023-24, as a part of that Mi ssion -- we have set up something called 
‘CMLRE’ which is Center for Marine Living  Resources and Ecology.  And that is a 
dedicated Center meant to take care of precisely the concerns which the hon’ble 
Member has expressed in this House today.   It is supposed to be a designated 
national repository for deep sea organisms (fauna).  Fauna is more of the animal kind 
of organisms which he is mentioning, be cause he is right.  There were no 
institutionalized safeguards, both of the Eastern Ghats as well as the Western Ghats.  
And, this Center is a part of this Deep  Ocean Mission which was launched by the 
hon’ble   Prime Minister and it is being coordinated by our Centre at Kochi.  Its 
mandate, broadly speaking, is the survey assessment and monitoring of the marine 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
63

 
 
life in deep seas, also to conduct research to find ways of their preservation and also 
to generate data as far as fisheries are con cerned, as far as the biodiversity hotspots 
are concerned, also the genetic databases, because, very soon, we will be adding 
that.  And, we have already launched another segment of it called ‘Bhavasagara’, 
which is a designated biodiversity referral center.  So, I am glad that the hon’ble 
Member has shared this very important info rmation with this august House, because 
this part of the subject does not come up often for discussion.  In other words, this is, 
I think, the first time that we have started looking at our ocean resources.  We have 
11,000 km. long coastal area, perhaps one of the longest in the world.  We are the 
only country which has an ocean called the Indian Ocean. वही सागर, वही महासागर,

whole country and the whole world will feel proud to come and invest.

the State capital.  Once that was decided, the foundation stone for the capital was

‘among the languages of the land, Telugu is the finest’, this adage was proclaimed by 
the Vijayanagara Emperor Sri Krishnadevara ya, the ruler of one of India's most 
magnificent kingdoms. He declared that the Telugu language is, indeed, the sweetest 
among all languages.  Furthermore, one of our renowned poets, Rayaprolu 
Subbarao, articulated this sentiment. "W herever you may travel, wherever you may 
set foot, whatever pedestal you may ascend, and whatever others may say, praise 
your motherland, Bharat, and uphold the fu ll dignity of your nation." The Telugu 
people are a community that has lived by an d proclaimed this very ideal. Hailing from 
such a glorious history, I, as a member of the Telugu community, truly bow my head 
in reverence to those farmers and moth ers who waged a prolonged and arduous 
struggle for the sake of Amaravati. 
Sir, as I rise to address this House regarding this Bill and as we move towards 
its eventual passage, it is impe rative that we also take a moment to recall its history, 
the history of the Telugu people, and also the legacy of the Telugu nation. In those 
days, when all Telugu-speaking people were living within the Madras Presidency, the 
immortal Potti Sreeramulu, a man of undying spirit, launched a movement demanding 
a separate Andhra State for the Telugu people. He undertook a hunger strike in 
pursuit of this cause. Yet, even after 50 days had elapsed, the Central Government in 
power at that time, a Congress administration, failed to offer even the slightest 
response or demonstrate even a shred of  humanity. Ultimately, after 58 days, the 
immortal Potti Sreeramulu sacrificed his very life for this cause. It was only in the wake 
of his demise and in response to his ultimate sacrifice that the Government of that era 
finally relented and established the Andhra State specifically for the Telugu people. 
Similarly, in 1956, during the reorganizati on of states along linguistic lines, the 
Government of the day sought to merge the region currently known as Telangana 
(then part of Hyderabad State) with the other Telugu-speaking areas. To facilitate 
this merger, the Government entered into a "Gentlemen's  Agreement" with various 
prominent figures, stipulating specific conditions. These conditions included 
guarantees regarding employment opportun ities for students and youth from the 
                   
& English translation of the original speech delivered in Telugu. 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
85

 
 
Telangana region, equitable distribution of  river waters, a fair share in the state 
budget, and proportionate representation in both political institutions and the 
administrative services. The Central Government of that era pledged to strictly uphold 
these terms and promised to safeguard the interests of the people of Telangana.  
However, in the aftermath of the merger, successive Governments completely 
disregarded these solemn promises. Adopting  a cynical attitude, akin to the proverb 
"Bodi Mallayya until the river is crossed, but Odi Mallayya once on the other side", 
the Congress Government of that time systematically reneged on its commitments. 
Ultimately, in 1969, when students and yo uth rose up to demand accountability 
regarding these unfulfilled promises, th e Congress Government responded with 
bullets, creating a tragic history in which 369 lives were lost.  This pattern of disregard 
continued between 1978 and 1982, during which the Congress Government treated 
the State of Andhra Pradesh with utter contempt. They changed Chief Ministers as 
frequently as one might change clothes, re placing the State's leadership once every 
year, resulting in four different Chief Mini sters within a span of four years. This 
conduct was deeply insulting to the Telugu  people and demeaned the entire Telugu 
community. When the late Anjaiah, who was then serving as Chief Minister, was 
publicly humiliated at Begu mpet Airport by the National General Secretary of the 
Congress Party, the entire Telangana community and society rose up in protest. 
Asserting their Telugu self-respect and dignit y, they declared that such disrespectful 
treatment by the "Delhi bosses" was unacceptable and unbecoming. Upholding the 
banner of self-respect, on that hist oric day, the world-renowned "Nata 
Sarvabhouma" (Emperor of Actors), Shri  N.T. Rama Rao, founded the Telugu 
Desam Party for the sake of the Telugu people. He rose to action in response to the 
manner in which the then-ruling Congress  Government had humiliated the Telugu 
nation and its people, an affront that the indomitable N.T. Rama Rao refused to 
tolerate. Although it was a regional part y, he ensured it remained a party that 
championed national interests. N.T. Rama Rao waged a relentless struggle against 
the policies of the Congress party; he brought together all non-Congress parties 
across the nation under a single umbrella, declaring that the Congress party's central 
hegemony would no longer be accepted.  Shri N.T. Rama Rao was a visionary who 
redefined politics at the national level. He ga ve a new definition to social justice within 
the political sphere, ensuring a rightful and dignified place for the marginalized, the 
weaker sections, the downtrodden, Dalits, and the SC, ST, and BC communities.  
Shri N.T. Rama Rao believed that true social justice meant delivering justice to the 
poor communities residing in rural areas, specifically those dependent on traditional 
hereditary occupations, such as the Letha, Geetha, Maccha, blacksmiths, potters, 
86
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
carpenters, washermen, barbers, and hunters.  Acting on this conviction, and as our 
colleague Shri Rammohan Naidu mentioned earlier, he brought numerous individuals 
from the most marginalized sections of the Backward Classes, figures like Yerran 
Naidu and Devender Goud, into the political  arena, thereby providing them with the 
opportunity to rise to the national stage.    Subsequently, when the Telugu Desam 
Party assumed power in Andhra Pradesh in 1995 under the leadership of Mr. 
Chandrababu Naidu, he emerged as a visi onary leader, a statesman with profound 
foresight, and strove tirelessly to foster  development in Hyde rabad. When I was 
elected as an MLA for the first time in 19 99, I had the privilege of working alongside 
him during his tenure as Chief Minister.  
On this occasion, I wish to emphasiz e that Mr. Chandrab abu Naidu’s pivotal 
role in transforming Hyderabad into "Cyberabad", a magnificent high-tech 
metropolis, and establishing it as a global IT hub today, is an achievement that is both 
inestimable and unforgettable. Furthermore, when the second phase of the 
Telangana movement emerged in 2001, the entire society and the Telugu people 
witnessed how the Congress party, which was in power at the time, failed to respond 
or show any compassion, even as 1,200 individuals sacrificed their lives in the struggle 
to achieve Statehood for Telangana. The Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) has 
consistently advocated for the creation of smaller States to ensure administrative 
convenience, a philosophy rooted in the idea that dividing into smaller units facilitates 
better governance than remaining as a single, massive entity. It was in this spirit that 
the party championed the movement for smaller States, ultimately achieving the 
formation of Telangana. Foll owing this bifurcation into two states in 2014, an NDA 
Government led by Shri Modi was formed at the Centre, while a corresponding NDA 
Government was established in the State of Andhra Pradesh.  Against this backdrop, 
as mentioned earlier, when the decision was taken to designate Amaravati as the new 
capital, the then Chief Minister, Mr. Ch andrababu Naidu, received the full and 
unequivocal support of Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi. During the inspiring 
foundation-laying ceremony held, as previo usly noted, on the banks of the Krishna 
River in the village of Uddandarayunipalem in Guntur district, Prime Minister Shri 
Narendra Modi demonstrated this commitme nt in a deeply symbolic manner. Acting 
in his capacity as Prime Minister, he persona lly carried sacred soil from the precincts 
of the Parliament in Delhi, along with holy waters from the Yamuna River, to the site.  
By doing so, he signaled that the Central Government would stand firmly in support of 
the project and remain fully committed to it. He declared that they would transform 
Amaravati into a world-class city, a "Greenfield City" that would serve as a beacon to 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]

the entire globe, and thus laid its founda tion. In this manner, Prime Minister Shri

identity of our Andhra Pradesh.  Amaravati is not just a place.  It is an idea, a vision,

SHRI PAKA VENKATA SATYANARAYANA (Andhra Pradesh) :  Thank you hon’ble 
Vice-Chairman, Sir, for giving me an opportunity.  I would like to represent the matter 
in my mother tongue, Telugu; so, kindly allow me to speak in Telugu.  I am from 
Andhra Pradesh.  &” Sir, when we examine the subject regarding the formation of 
States within India, it is a well-known fact to all of us that the nation, which initially 
comprised approximately 15 States, subseque ntly evolved into a union of 29 States. 
In every instance, where a new State was formed, and specifically due to the 
decisions   taken by the respective Central Governments regarding the circumstances 
of such formation, we have witnessed, over the past 70 years, the difficulties and 
hardships that the concerned State Governments had to endure.   In this context, I 
wish to make the ideological stance of the Congress Party very clear. Initially, in 1956, 
when the southern states of this country, Kerala, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, and Andhra 
Pradesh, were all part of a unified 'Southern Province,' it was Potti Sreeramulu who 
spearheaded the struggle for the formation of linguistic states. It was due to the 
policies adopted by the Congress party, of which he was a member, that he 
ultimately lost his life; indeed, it was his supreme sacrifice that paved the way for the 
clear realization of states organized along linguistic lines. Prior to this, Tanguturi 
Prakasam Pantulu, despite being a member of the Congress party, defied the party 
leadership to establish his own regional part y and upon coming to power in the state, 
the policies he implemented were instrument al in shaping the very contours of what 
would become Andhra Pradesh. 
In the process, the Andhra region lost co ntrol over several of its territories. For 
instance, we lost Berhampur to Orissa, and Bellary to Karnataka, similarly, parts of 
the Karimnagar region were ceded to Maharashtra. Having suffered such territorial 
losses under various circumstances, the state of Andhra Pradesh was formally 
constituted in 1956. History records that, at the time of its formation, and in 
accordance with specific recommendations,  Kurnool was designated and recognized 
as the capital city.  In the period following 1956, driven by the linguistic principle that 
all Telugu-speaking regions should be united, and acting upon the request of Burgula 
Ramakrishna Rao from the Telangana region, a pivotal decision was taken. This 
decision involved dismantling the Nizam' s rule in Hyderabad, integrating the 
Hyderabad region into the State of Andhra  Pradesh, thereby bringing the Nizam's 
assets under state control, and establishing Hyderabad as the capital city. This 
remains a matter of historical fact well kn own to us all. Howeve r, after thoroughly

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
examining every aspect of this issue, one fa ct has emerged with absolute clarity. The 
immense difficulties currently being faced by the state of Andhra Pradesh stem 
directly from the Central Government's failure to make a sound decision regarding the 
reorganization of states. I wish to make this point unequivocally clear through this 
platform. The root cause lies in an ill-consid ered decision taken in 2014, driven solely 
by electoral ambitions, which lacked clarity and foresight. At that time, the leader of a 
regional party, the Telangana Rashtra Samithi (TRS), incited animosity and created 
various obstacles between the Andhra and Telangana regions; it was against this 
backdrop that the Central Government, then led by the Congress party, proceeded to 
bifurcate the state. 
The unfortunate reality is that the political parties of that era were preoccupied 
with only two opposing viewpoints: one advocating for bifurcation, and the other 
opposing it. They failed to engage in cr itical thinking regarding the practical 
consequences, specifically, "What would we require if the State were to be 
divided?", or, conversely, they remained  under the complacent assumption that 
"Under no circumstances will the State be divided." Consequently, the future of 
Andhra Pradesh became shrouded in uncertai nty, leading to the precarious situation 
in which the state finds itself today. 
Furthermore, it is a matter of public record that following the State bifurcation 
in 2014, and based on the political unde rstanding reached between the then-ruling 
Telugu Desam Party and the Bharatiya Jana t a  P a r t y ,  w e  p r o ceeded to establish 
Amaravati as the new capital city, under th e leadership of Shri Nara Chandrababu 
Naidu. However, pursuant to Article 3,  the Central Government possesses the 
discretionary power to create any number of states. Yet, given that the Indian 
Constitution explicitly vests the authority to select a State's capital solely in the 
respective State Government, the administration at that time specifically designated 
this particular region as the capital. Regrettably, the current predicament has arisen 
due to a systemic flaw wherein the Governme nt's vision and policies tend to shift 
whenever there is a change in the ruling poli tical party. The crucial distinction lies in 
the fact that, back then, the people of the Amaravati region, driven by their faith in the 
incumbent administration and their imme nse trust in Mr. Chandrababu Naidu, 
voluntarily surrendered approximately 30, 000 acres of land under a land pooling 
scheme. However, the subsequent Government, instead of upholding and continuing 
that vision, approached the matter with a vindictive mindset, an attitude that 
threatens to unleash massive turmoil in the days to come.   The critical situation that 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
115

 
 
both the Central and State Governments must confront is the grave danger that the 
public stands to lose its faith in the government. 
We have clearly witnessed today the detrimental consequences that arise 
when people entrust their assets to indi viduals they trust, only for subsequent 
Governments to view the decisions made by their predecessors solely through a 
political lens. This is evident in the situation in Amaravati, where, for nearly five years, 
men, women, and children alike have enga ged in a unique movement. They have 
stood their ground, enduring numerous Go vernment-imposed restrictions, various 
illegal cases, and a multitude of hardships. 
The outcome of the 2024 elections has made it abundantly clear that the 
sentiments of the people on the ground hold far greater priority than the agendas and 
manifestos of political parties. This fundamental principle, that public sentiment drives 
the transfer of power, is the reason why the electorate of this State effectively wiped 
out the YSRCP, limiting their legislative strength to a mere 11 seats. It is imperative 
that we understand this underlying dynami c regarding the shift in governance within 
the state.  Regrettably, in the slogans they raised just moments ago, they merely 
stated that they "oppose". I had hoped that they might, at the very least, offer a 
constructive "proposal." However, by me rely offering opposition, the YSRCP has 
once again placed itself in a position wher e it is compelled to provide answers to the 
public in the political arena. Furthermore, we have clearly witnessed today the 
undeniable reality that Andhra Pradesh remains a State with a designated capital city.

is addressing the gathering. It has been two years since the State was bifurcated.

Andhra Pradesh was 1,450 TMCs. However, at no point during the era of the united 
State did we ever utilize more than 650 TMCs . Given this situation of underutilization, 
the construction of the Polavaram project has become an absolute necessity today. If 
the stipulated height required for the Polavara m project is reduced, or if the project is 
stalled, it creates a volatile situation th at risks reigniting animosity between the 
regions. Any attempt to reduce the proj ect's scope is bound to be viewed and 
debated through a political lens. It is pr ecisely because the state bifurcation was 
executed without due consideration for such critical circumstances that this 
predicament has arisen, resulting in the im mense pressure currently weighing upon 
these governments. Therefore, it is imperative that we overcome all these challenges.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Next, Shrimati Priyanka Chaturvedi.    
SHRIMATI PRIYANKA CHATURVEDI (Maharashtra) : Mr. Chairman, Sir.  I am happy 
that I stand to make my last speech, for th is term, in solidarity for the people of 
Andhra Pradesh, especially the women and the farmers of Amravati for the long-
awaited justice they sought from this countr y. It was heartening to hear the words of 
124
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Civil Aviation Minister, Kinjarapu Rammohan Naidu Ji.  His words carried the weight of 
the emotions, and not just the emotions but also the pride that the people of Andhra 
Pradesh carry in their hearts for their State.  It was extremely emotional for me to hear 
the words that he spoke.  So, I commend him for that.   It is also heartening to see 
the entire team of the TDP, sitting right th ere, listening to every part of everyone’s 
speech.   
Sir, today we take up a Bill that is, at its heart, an admission of failure - a 
belated reckoning with what was done to the people of Andhra Pradesh in 2014. 
When this Parliament passed the Andhra Pr adesh Reorganisation Bill, it carved out a 
new State and left it, quite literally, without a capital, without a Secretariat and without 
an administrative ecosystem. While Telangana was given Hyderabad, a ready, 
globally- recognised city, An dhra Pradesh was given a promise. Today, we are here 
to put a name to that promise. And, while we welcome that, this House must not 
allow itself to celebrate without also accounti ng for the long injustice. Sir, we support 
the principle of giving Andhra Pradesh a permanent, statutory capital. Amaravati was 
identified after consultation and planning, and significant infrastructure investment has 
already begun. Over 56,000 crore worth of projects are still underway. But, nearly 
34,000 acres of agricultural land was po oled from farmers who surrendered their 
livelihoods on the faith of a promise, developed plots, housing, and education for 
their children. This Bill, as it stands, contains no timeline, no compensation 
framework and no binding commitment to those farmers.  Sir, I am hoping that this 
will also be looked into.  
Sir, when Andhra Pradesh was bifurcated, the Central Government made 
explicit assurances about Special Category  status, a designation that would have 
meant higher Central Grants, tax concessi ons and development support for a newly 
formed, economically- disadvantaged State. Those assurances were, unfortunately, 
not honoured. The BJP has been in power at the Centre for over 12 years, and the 
State still awaits what was promised to it in letter and spirit. This Bill gives Amaravati a 
name in the statute books. 
(MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair.) 
But it should get the confidence from the Ce ntral Government. Sir, this Bill will pass 
and it should pass. But Parliament must not walk away from Andhra Pradesh the 
moment this vote is done, just as it e ffectively walked away in 2014. I urge the 
Government, do not let this amendment be the full stop on Andhra Pradesh's 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]

aspirations, its people’s aspirations, their pride, their resilience; follow through on 
special status commitments, ensure water treaty obligations are met and protect the 
farmers who gave their land and the women who fought for this cause.  Thank you so 
much, Sir.  I stand in total solidarity with this Bill.  Thank you.

All India Trinamool Congress, I would like to thank my leader Ms. Mamata Banerjee 
for this opportunity. This one Bill is amending nearly 80 Central Acts. 80! Why have all 
the criminal provisions been merged into one law? Every Act serves a different 
purpose and context. What is the approach of the Government? Once again, the 
Government has done what it does best: Undermine legislative spirit and intent. The 
Secretary of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry wrote a letter to the Chairperson 
of the Select Committee on the Jan Vish was (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 2026 
asking to widen the scope of the legislation and for more Acts to be examined. With 
what authority is the executive asking the Chairman of the Select Committee to make 
changes? Since when is one arm of the legislature taking orders from the executive? 
The executive is beyond its limits to ask such a thing. It is only the Speaker of the 
House, which has the authority, to examine what can be and what cannot be 
considered by the Select Committee. Why wa s such a major Bill with a significant 
volume of amendments brought in after the entire Session has passed? We cannot 
even claim today is the last day of the Session. The Government has kept us in the 
dark even about that.   
SHRI JAIRAM RAMESH (Karnataka): Today is not the last day.
