SHRI P. WILSON (Tamil Nadu) : Madam Vice-Chairman, I rise today not merely to 
oppose, but to vehemently resist this dang erous, deeply flawed and utterly reckless 
piece of legislation, the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 2026. Let me 
s a y  t h i s  a t  t h e  o u t s e t  w i t h o u t  m i n c i n g  w o r d s .  T h i s  B i l l  i s  n o t  r e f o r m ,  i t  i s  n o t  
progressive governance, it is a calculated dismantling of deterrence, systematic 
dilution of accountability and a brazen surre nder of the rule of law at the altar of 
corporate convenience. What is being sold as trust-based governance is in truth a 
pay and escape regime where the powerful  can violate the law and walk away by 
writing a cheque. In August 2025,  the Government introduced the Jan Vishwas  
(Amendment) Bill, 2025 seeking to amend 355 provisions across 16 Central Acts 
administered by ten ministries. The Bill wa s rightly referred to a Select Committee. 
The Committee recommended decriminaliz ing across 62 additional Central Acts. 
Following this, the 2025 Bill was withdrawn, but instead of exercising restraint, the 
Government has returned today with an ev en more sweeping and liberal version, the 
Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 2026 to help the corporate friends. This 
new Bill now seeks to amend 80 Central Acts , decriminalize staggering 717 offenses 
and modify 784 provisions spanning 23 mini stries. The Bill is pushed through this 
House without stakeholder consultation, without engagement with States and without 
public scrutiny. Why is there a tearing hurry to move this Bill? Whom do you want to 
save? The first and foremost glaring flaw is  the blanket decriminalization of offences 
without any rational classification . There is no distinction between minor procedural 
lapses and serious violations that endanger human life. Everything is treated as 
technical and swept under the same umbrella. This one-size-fits-all approach is not a 
reform. It is a legislative irresponsibility. It ignores the ground reality that not all 
offences are equal and some demand strict deterrence. Take the Drugs and 
Cosmetics Act, 1940. Earlier, manufacturi ng or selling substandard or harmful 
cosmetics could lead to imprisonment of one year. Now, a fine, just a fine! So let us 
understand what this means in real life. A company manufactures toxic skin products, 
women suffer burns, children develop infection, liver, kidney, brain are affected by the 
products. Lives are permanently scarred and what happens to the offender? He pays 
a penalty, they continue business and cont inue to make profit! Madam, this is not 
decriminalization. The victims, ordinary ci tizens, bear the irreversible consequences, 
while violators treat penalties as cost of doing business.  Madam, the Food Safety 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
141

 
 
Standards Act is compromised.  The steali ng of electricity and severe provisions 
under the Electricity Act, 2003, is diluted.  The builder under the Real Estate 
Regulation and Development Act, 2016, will not comply and will not give a 
constructed flat.  The clinical test and di agnosis will not take seriously as Clinical 
Establishment Act, 2010, decriminalizes with  penalty.  Fake stamps can be sold and 
only penalty is charged.  Now, you can thr eaten officers of Defence and only penalty 
is leviable as the Works of Defence Act is diluted.  Now, you can steal copyright and 
walk away with penalty.  Public safety is similarly compromised under laws like 
National Highways Act, 1956.  Acts that  make highways unsafe or impassable, 
potentially causing fatal accident, delaying ambulances and endangering thousands 
of lives, earlier attracted imprisonment up  to five years.  Now, this has been 
converted into a monetary penalty ranging from rupees ten lakhs to one crore.  A 
corporate entity can block a highway, disrupt emergency services, cause accident 
and settle the matter financially.  Deterrence disappears entirely.  What message are 
we sending?  The same pattern is visi ble across transport laws, including the 
Railways Framework.  Offences such as drunken and disorderly behavior, 
endangering passengers, trespassing, smoking in prohibited areas and even 
violations affecting women's safety like entering reserved compartments are diluted to 
fines or reduced penalty.  The message is  deeply troubling that public order, 
passenger safety and women's dignity can be compromised without serious 
consequences.  Environmental protection is  also significantly weakened.  Across 
multiple laws, offences relating to air pollution, noise pollution and discharge from the 
inland vessels are CC, imprisonment removed or reduced.  Polluters can continue 
harmful activities, pay fines and carry on operations.  At a time when environmental 
degradation is a major public health crisis, this approach prioritizes convenience over 
sustainability.  The impact extends to rural and labor sectors as well.  Under the 
Cattle Trespass Act, 1871, damage to farmer's crops caused by stray cattle is now 
reduced to a fine only; offence leaving farm ers with little real remedy.  Labor law 
violations relating to dock workers, prov ident funds and employment conditions are 
also diluted into a monetary penalty, weakening already fragile protection for workers.  
Madam, are we in a jungle raj or should there be a rule of law? 
The second major flaw is the structural shift from judicial courts to executing 
adjudicating officers.  Penalties will no w be imposed by bureaucrats rather than 
independent judges.  Appeals are limited and often remain with the same 
administrative hierarchy.  This removes ju dicial oversight, concentrates power with 
the executive… …(Time-bell rings)… Madam, I will just take two minutes to finish it off.  
142
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
And, the executive, increasing the risk of arbitrariness and misuse.  It replaces due 
process with discretion and transparency with opacity.   
Third, the Bill creates two tiered systems of justice.  For large corporations, 
penalties become manageable financial liab ilities.  For ordinary citizens and small 
business, the same system can be burden some and unpredictable.  The Bill even 
provides for an automatic enhancement of fines by 10 per cent every three years.  
Yet, this is unlikely to act as a deterrent for entities with significant financial resources.  
Violation becomes budgeted expenses, wh ile justice becomes unequal in this 
application.   
Fourth, the Bill omits certain offences entirely, including false fire alarms under 
the Delhi Police Framework, failure to repo rt births and deaths, false entries in 
copyright registers, while they ma y appear minor individually.  …( Time-bell rings )…   
One minute, Madam, I will finish.  Remova l, weakness, accountability, data, integrity 
and public order are viewed collectively.  The Government claims that this Bill reduces 
the burden on courts and eliminates Inspec tor Raj.  However, over 7,300 criminal 
provisions already existing across criminal  laws, the decriminalization 717 offences 
without detailed impact assessment is deeply concerning. The earlier Select 
Committee has flagged issues such as lack of safeguards, inadequate penalty 
structures and absence of classification.  These concerns remain unaddressed.  
Madam, laws are not meant to be convenient for violators.  They are meant to protect 
citizens.  Deterrence is not an obstacle.  It is an essential to maintaining order and 
accountability.  When the fear of meanin gful consequences removed, compliance 
weakens, violations increase.  This Bill ri sks sending a message that compliance is 
optional, violation can be monetized and the accountability can be negotiated.  It 
shifts the focus from the justice to revenue, from protection to convenience.  
Therefore, I urge that the Bill may not be pass ed in haste.  Let it be referred to a Joint 
Parliamentary Committee for a comprehensive review.  Stakeholders, including 
States, experts, regulators and civil societ y must be consulted. The framework must 
be reworked to include clear classificatio n of offences, proportionate penalties and 
adequate judicial oversight of serious violations.   The responsibility of this House is to 
protect the people of the country.  Any reform must strengthen, not dilute, the 
principles of justice, accountability and public safety.  Thank you very much.

Shri Golla Baburao.  You have five minutes.  
SHRI GOLLA BABURAO (Andhra Pradesh) : Madam Vice-Chairperson. I rise to 
speak on the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 2026. It is a significant 
step towards reforming our regulatory legal framework. At its core, the Bill seeks to 
replace the culture of criminalization for minor offences with one rooted in trust and 
ease of compliance. It reflects an important shift in governance, where the aim is to 
balance enforcement with the needs of a modern growing economy.  Having said 
this, I would first like to speak about its achievements. As decriminalization reduces 
fear and unlocks economic activity, the key positive of the Jan Vishwas Bill, 2026, is 
the large-scale decriminalization of minor technical offences. By removing the threat 
of imprisonment for procedural lapses, such as filing delays or documentation errors, 
the Bill creates a more enabling enviro nment for business, especially MSMEs and 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
145

 
 
start-ups. Entrepreneurs are more likely to  take risks and expand operations when 
compliance mistakes no longer carry criminal consequences. The shift from a 
punishment-based to a trust-based regu latory framework improves investor 
sentiment, encourages formalization of enterprises, and aligns India's legal 
architecture with global best practices in business regulation and economic 
governance. It also reduces litigation and enables faster dispute resolution. 
The Bill significantly eases the burden on  India's already overburdened judicial 
system by shifting minor offences from crim inal courts to administrative adjudication. 
Instead of lengthy trials, such cases can no w be resolved through designated officers 
and monetary penalties. It also allows courts to focus on serious criminal matters 
rather than routine compliance violations.  In a country like India, where 4.87 crore 
cases are currently pending, of which about 18 per cent have been pending for over 
10 years and nearly 20 per cent between 5 to 10 years, this reform is particularly 
significant. It strengthens institutional capacity, enhances regulatory clarity, and 
contributes to a more predictable an d time-bound enforcement ecosystem.  
However, some concerns re main. While the Bill seeks to decriminalize several 
offences by replacing impris onment with civil penalties, in some cases the penalty 
amounts remain unchanged and outdated. For instance, under the NDMC Act, 1994, 
fines ranging from ₹25 to ₹100 have been retained at the same level despite being 
introduced over three decades ago.  Unrevi sed penalties may, therefore, weaken the 
overall deterrence. Similarly, offences such as failure to give notice of transfer of 
property and failure to disclose liability co ntinue with unchanged civil penalties under 
the Bill. In other instances, the Bill neithe r decriminalises the of fence nor revises the 
fine. For example, interference with a muni cipal drain under the NDMC Act, 1994, still 
carries a fine of ₹50, and has been unchanged since 1994.  There is a need for a 
standardised framework to ensu re consistency in penalties . The most important is, 
there is a risk of increased administrative discretion; and, hence, there is a need for 
safeguards. This makes it essential to build strong safeguards into the system. Clear 
guidelines, transparent procedures, and effective appellate mechanisms are 
necessary to ensure fairness and accounta bility. Without such protections, the 
intended benefits of efficiency and ease of compliance may be undermined by 
uncertainty and misuse.  
The next is limited focus on strengthening compliance capacity. (Time-bell 
rings) Madam, give me one minute.  While th e Bill reduces penalties and simplifies 
enforcement, it does not sufficiently address the deeper issue of compliance 
capacity, especially among MSMEs. India has over 6.3 crore MSMEs, many of which 
146
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
struggle not with intent, but with unders tanding and managing complex regulations. 
With these negative points, I request that this  B i l l  b e  s e n t  t o  a  S e l e c t  C o m m i t t e e .    
Thank you.

SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha) : Madam Vice-Chairperson, I thank you for giving 
me an opportunity to speak on the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 
2026.  First of all, I would like to say- Can public trust or Jan Vishwas be built merely 
by amending provisions, or does it requir e accountability, justice, and sincerity in 
governance?  Madam, the Government claims that the Jan Vishwas Bill will 
decriminalize minor offences and improve Ease of Doing Business. On paper, it 
sounds progressive. But on the ground, especially in States like Odisha, reality is very 
different because small MSME traders, farmers, forest dwellers, SCs, STs and OBC 
people, women, innocent citizens are still harassed by excessive regulations and 
bureaucratic control continues to dominate, while accountability remains weak. So, I 
ask: Is this Bill truly about Jan Vishwas or public trust or only about easing 
compliance for big entities? Why are tribal communities penalized for accessing forest 
resources, which are their traditional rights? We decriminalize on one side, but on the 
other side, systems continue to criminalize poverty and survival. This contradiction 
must be addressed. As regards the impact on Tribal India, again, it has been ignored. 
Madam, I come from Odisha, a land of rich  tribal heritage. For tribal communities, 
forest produce is not a “commodity”, it is life and livelihood. Minor procedural 
violations should not lead to penalties or fear of law. But unfortunately, the system still 
treats them as offenders instead of rightful stakeholders. The Bill should have explicit 
safeguards for the Scheduled Tribes, the Scheduled Castes and Backward 
communities; protection under Forest Rights framework and clear instructions to 
prevent administrative harassment.  With out this, ‘Jan Vishwas’ becomes just a 
slogan.  I want to ask:  What about ease of living for the poor?  I am asking this 
because farmers are struggling with the pr oblems of paddy procurement token and 
getting MSP and input subsidy in the State of Odisha. While tribal people collect 
forest produce for their livelihood, they are being implicated in false cases by the 
Forest Department.   If they oppose any mi ning project or dam project on their forest 
land or on their raiyati land, the police implicate them in false cases and send  
them to jail.

A small entrepreneur who is facing inspect ion, for them, the fear of law is still 
real. If trust has to be built, it must begin from the last person in the queue. True Jan

safeguards for poor, for farmers, for wo men, for people belonging to Scheduled

THE VICE- CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI RAJANI ASHOKRAO PATIL):  Now, Shri 
Masthan Rao Yadav Beedha.  
SHRI MASTHAN RAO YADAV BEEDHA (Andhra Pradesh) :  Madam, on behalf of our 
Telugu Desam Party and our leader, Shri Nara Chandrababu Naidu Garu, I rise to 
speak on the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Prov isions) Bill, 2026.  This Bill reflects a 
progressive shift in governance.  It aims to reduce fear of criminal penalties, promote 
trust-based compliance and create a more business-friendly and citizen-friendly 
regulatory environment across the country. Let me mention the benefits of the Bill. 
The Jan Vishwas Bill, 2026 is a landmark reform that focuses on decriminalization and 
rationalization of laws. It amends around 80 Central Acts and addresses more than 
152
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
1,100 offences by replacing criminal punishme nt with civil penalties. First, the Bill 
removes unnecessary criminal provisions. Ma ny minor and technical offences, which 
earlier carried imprisonment, will now attract only monetary penalties. This reduces 
the burden on the courts and prevents ha rassment of citizens and businesses for 
procedural lapses.  Second, the Bill improves Ease of Doing Business. By 
decriminalizing 717 provisions, it sends a strong message to investors that India trusts 
the entrepreneurs. Businesses can now operate with greater confidence without fear 
of imprisonment for minor e rrors. This reform will encourage investment, innovation 
and job creation. Third, the Bill introduces  a graded approach to compliances. For 
many offences, the first instance will attract an advisory, the second a warning, and 
only repeated violations will lead to penalt ies.   Fourth, the Bill modernizes penalties. 
Fifth, it introduces improvement notices and adjudication mechanisms.  Instead of 
immediate punishment, individuals and businesses are given time to correct their 
mistakes. By removing outdated offences  and simplifying procedures, it reduces 
unnecessary legal burden on ordinary citize ns. It reflects a governance model based 
on trust, transparency and efficiency.  
Hon’ble Vice-Chairperson, Madam, coming from the State of Andhra Pradesh, 
I would like to highlight an initiative undertaken by hon’ble  Chief Minister, Nara 
Chandrababu Naidu Garu, that has led to our State emerging as a leader in 
investments and growth, that is, the ‘Speed of Doing Business’. This approach 
ensures that investors do not face delays  and uncertainties.  Our State emphasizes 
transparency, accountability and time-bo und clearances, leading to increased 
investor confidence and positioned our State as a competitive investment destination. 
This model aligns perfectly with the spirit of Jan Vishwas Bill …( Time bell rings)… I am 
concluding, Madam.  It demonstrates how reforms at the ground level can translate 
into real economic growth and employment.  In conclusion, I would say that the Jan 
Vishwas Bill, 2026 is a forward-lookin g reform that strengthens trust among 
Government, citizens and businesses.  With  these words, I support the Bill.  Thank 
you.

महोदया , मȅ अपनी गुजराती भाषा मȂ बोलना चाहता हूँ।  “Hon’ble  Chairperson Madam, 
Greetings. I would like to speak in my mo ther tongue, Gujarati . Jay Bajaranga Bali, 
Jay Shri Marutinandana!  
Today is the day of Shri Bajarangabali.  On this occasion, through you Madam, 
I would like to extend greetings to the hon’ble Members of this august House and the 
citizens of our country. I have a lot of faith in Shri Hanuma nji. Madam, I rise today to 
express my strong and unwavering suppo rt for the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of 
Provisions) Bill, 2026—a visionary and transfor mative legislative initiative, introduced 
under the visionary leadership of our hon’ble  Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, and 
taken forward under the guidance of hon’ble  Minister, Shri Piyush Goyal.  
This Bill is not just a change in law; it is an attempt to redefine the relationship 
between the Government and the citizens—a path of trust instead of suspicion and 
reform instead of rigidity. Madam, the essence of this Jan Vishwas Bill is anchored in 
the principle of “Minimum Government, Maximum Governance ”. For decades, our 
legal system was riddled with outdated and irrelevant provisions that criminalized even 
minor and technical errors. This created fear among ordinary citizens and traders, 
discouraged entrepreneurship and increased  pressure on the judiciary. The Jan 
Vishwas Bill, 2026 is an important step toward s removing this legacy and creating a 
modern, practical and citizen-centric legal framework. Madam, this Jan Vishwas Bill 
is the result of democratic debate and deliberation. The in itial Bill introduced in 2025 
included only 17 laws. But after the recommendations of the Lok Sabha Select 
Committee—chaired by Shri Tejashwi Surya—this scope has been increased to 80 
Central Acts. This is a testament to the government’s commitment to open thinking 
and dialogue. The most important aspe ct of this Jan Vishwas Bill is—
decriminalization of crimes, i.e., removing many minor errors fr om the category of 
crimes. For example, under the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, some offences were 
punishable by imprisonment, but now they have been replaced by monetary fines. 
Similarly, under the National Highways Act,  1956, some offences are punishable by 
heavy fines instead of imprisonment. This reflects a more bala nced and equitable 
approach. Hon’ble Chairperson Madam, in some cases, imprisonment has been 
completely abolished. In laws like the Indi an Succession Act, 1925 and the Electricity 
Act, 2003, only fines will be imposed. This is a humane and practical approach, which 
avoids unnecessary imprisonment. In addi tion, this Jan Vishwas Bill has removed 
several old and unnecessary offences—such as raising a false fire alarm or not giving 
                   
 English translation of original speech in Gujarati. 
154
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
birth or death information. Such provisions  ha ve  bec om e  irre le vant in today's times 
and unnecessarily complicate the law. Madam, the amendment in fines and penalties 
is also an important part of this Jan Vi shwas Bill. Many fines have now been made 
time-sensitive and a provision has been made for an increase of 10% every three 
years, so that they remain effective. This  Jan Vishwas Bill  also has a system of 
warnings and advice. Advice for the first mistake, warning for the second and only 
then fine—this approach is not punitive but corrective, which encourages voluntary 
compliance. The idea of “correction notice” under the Legal Metrology Act, 2009 is 
also very progressive. This gives people an d traders an opportunity to correct their 
mistakes, which helps in building trust and cooperation. This public confidence Bill 
also has a system of statutory officers and appellate powers to strengthen the 
decision-making process, which will ensu re speedy and transparent justice. We

Minister Shri Narendra Modi. From Ease  of Doing Business reforms to digital 
governance—this Government is constantly striving to empower citizens. Hon’ble 
Minister, Shri Piyush Goyal has been instrumental in taking these reforms forward. His 
commitment and vision have shaped this Jan Vishwas Bill.

amendment—it is an idea, a message that the Government has faith in its citizens. It 
symbolizes trust instead of fear, cooperat ion instead of punishment, and simplicity 
instead of complexity. I wholeheartedly supp ort this Jan Vishwas Bill and urge all the 
members of this august House to pass it unanimously. Thank you. जय बजरंग बली!

Concern over non-implementation of livestock promotion schemes for the pastoral 
community of Jammu and Kashmir 
SHRI GULAM ALI (Nominated) :  Sir, the Gujjar-Bakarwal community is a traditional 
pastoral group whose primary livelihood  depends on rearing sheep and goats. 
Livestock forms the backbone of their economy and sustenance. Therefore, 
168
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Government schemes promoting livestock de velopment are critically important for 
their welfare. The Government has introduced programmes such as the National 
Livestock Mission and the Holistic Rural Development Programme to enhance 
productivity, commercialization and inco me of livestock-dependent communities. 
However, despite their relevance, the Gu jjar-Bakarwal community has largely been 
unable to access these benefits, particularly  in the Jammu division. District targets 
were fixed for establishing li vestock units, yet applications from this community are 
often ignored or delayed. Under the Public Good category of 525 units, not a single 
unit has been allotted to members of this community. Although procedures are 
simple, applications are not processed efficiently, leading to exclusion from schemes 
meant for their upliftment.  A district-w ise review of beneficiaries should be 
conducted.  Special facilitation camps mu st be organized in pastoral areas. Time-
bound processing mechanisms and accountability measures should be enforced. 
Priority consideration for pastoral commun ities may also be introduced. Ensuring 
equitable access is essential for socio-economic upliftment and sustainable livestock 
development in the region. This will help br idge gaps in implemen tation, ensure fair 
representation, strengthen livelihoods, re duce marginalization, improve trust in 
institutions and enable the community to fu lly participate in development initiatives 
and national growth processes effectively and sustainably going forward ahead. I urge 
Government to look into it. Thank you. 
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN : The following hon’ble Members associated themselves

Concern over the difficulties faced by tobacco farmers in Andhra Pradesh 
SHRI YERRAM VENKATA SUBBA REDDY (Andhra Pradesh):  Sir, Andhra Pradesh 
accounts for about 22 per cent of India’s total tobacco production, making it one of 
the largest producing States in the country. Tobacco farmers in districts such as 
Prakasam, Nellore and parts of the Godavari region, are facing mounting distress due 
to declining auction prices, reduced procurement and the impact of recent tax and 
regulatory changes affecting the legal tobacco market. Thousands of small and 
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
169

 
 
marginal farmers dependent on FCV (Flue- cured Virginia) tobacco cultivation have 
expressed concern over falling returns, ri sing input costs and growing uncertainty 
about future viability. While public health objectives remain paramount, the livelihoods 
of lakhs of rural families engaged in tobacco cultivation an d allied activities must also 
be safeguarded. Many farmers have limited access to viable alternative crops due to 
soil suitability, established credit linkages and existing market infrastructure. Abrupt 
market contraction without transition support risks pushing farmers into indebtedness 
and economic instability. 
In view of the above, I urge the Government to consider a comprehensive 
support mechanism, including price stabil ization measures, credit restructuring 
assistance, export facilitation and a phased crop diversification programme with 
assured market linkage. A balanced approach  is essential to protect rural livelihoods 
while upholding national public health priorities. Thank you.

Need for regulation of online matrimonial and dating platforms 
MS. SWATI MALIWAL (National Capital Territory of Delhi) :  Sir, I seek to raise an 
issue affecting thousands across our countr y - the lack of regulation of online 
matrimonial and dating platforms.  These platforms have helped many find true 
companionship and life partners.  However, a deeply concerning pattern is emerging.  
In Hyderabad, a 42-year-old widower, a man,  who had already lost his wife, went on 
a matrimonial platform hoping to rebuild his life.  Instead, he was emotionally 
manipulated and lured into a fake crypto in vestment leading to an ultimate loss of 
Rs.86 lakh.  In Delhi, a man was recently arrested for duping over 700 women on 
dating apps by posing as a US-based model.  And, these are not isolated cases.  
Even the Government employees, professionals, educated people all are falling victim 
to fraud, blackmail and even violence throug h these sites.  Anyone can create a fake 
profile.  There is no mandatory identity ve rification, no accountability of platforms and 
no real-time fraud detection.  These companies earn crores in profit, but when fraud 
happens, they simply say “Use our site at your own risk.”  If we do not act now, these 
platforms will become hunting grounds for criminals.  I, therefore, demand the 
following from the Government : (i) Mandatory KYC verification for all users of 
matrimonial and dating sites. (ii) Legal liabil ity of platforms in case of fraud. (iii) AI-
based monitoring of suspicious behavi or. (iv) Immediate grievance redressal 
mechanisms. (v) Strong coordination with cybercrime agencies.  People come to 
these sites seeking trust and companionship, and rightfully so.  They must not leave 
with trauma and loss.  The time for stronger safeguards is now.  I urge the 
Government to look into this.            
[ 02 April, 2026 ]
